# Principles of Economics and Management: question papers in full

127 unique papers · 2013-14 to 2025-26 · Information Technology, Data Science, Computer Engineering, Mechanical, Civil, Artificial Intelligence, Common (all branches), Cyber Security, Electronics and Telecommunication, AI and Machine Learning, Mechatronics, AI and Data Science, Computer Science, Chemical

Each paper: year, exam, semester, departments and programmes, then its full text as extracted. Scanned papers were read by OCR, so equations and symbols may contain recognition errors. Structured data: [`questions.jsonl`](questions.jsonl) (one record per question) and [`papers.jsonl`](papers.jsonl) (one record per paper). Index of files: [`README.md`](README.md).

a. | Law of supply and its exceptions
The Law of Supply states that, all else being equal, an increase in the
price of a good or service leads to an increase in the quantity supplied,
as producers are motivated by higher profits. However, there are
CO- 1; BL- ; ‘ ; : d :
exceptions to this law, including Giffen goods, where demand rises [5]
Understand

despite price increases due to inferior goods, and Veblen goods, where
higher prices enhance their status appeal. Additionally, in certain
situations like natural disasters or shortages, suppliers may not be able
to increase output despite rising prices, challenging the typical behavior
described by the Law of Supply.

CO-1; BL- Remember | b. | Monopolistic Competition

[5]
--- page 2 ---
Monopolistic competition is a market structure characterized by a large
number of firms that sell similar but not identical products, allowing for
product differentiation. Each firm has some degree of market power,
enabling them to set prices above marginal cost. This competition leads
to a variety of choices for consumers and promotes innovation.
However, in the long run, economic profits tend to be zero as new
entrants are attracted to the market, driving prices down to the level of

average costs.

c. | Delegation of Authorities

Delegation of authority refers to the process by which a manager assigns
responsibility and decision-making power to subordinates to enhance
operational efficiency and empower employees. This practice
CO-3; BL- encourages accountability and fosters a sense of ownership among team
Understand members, enabling quicker decision-making and improved productivity. Bl
Effective delegation also allows managers to focus on strategic tasks
while developing the skills and capabilities of their team. However, it
requires clear communication and trust to ensure that delegated tasks

align with organizational goals.

d. | Functions of a Marketing Manager

A marketing manager is responsible for developing and implementing
marketing strategies to promote products and services effectively. Key
functions include conducting market research to understand consumer

CO- 3; BL- Analyze needs, analyzing competition, and identifying target markets. They | [5]
oversee advertising and promotional campaigns, manage the marketing
budget, and track campaign performance to optimize results.
Additionally, marketing managers collaborate with cross-functional
teams to ensure cohesive messaging and brand consistency across’ all

ee channels, ultimately driving sales and enhancing customer engagement.
--- page 3 ---
Q2 [20]

a. | Apply the concept of the Production Possibility Curve (PPC) to explain
how a country can achieve economic growth.

The Production Possibility Curve (PPC) illustrates the trade-offs
between the production of two goods in an economy, demonstrating the
maximum output attainable given fixed resources and technology.
Economic growth is depicted as an outward shift of the PPC, which
indicates an increase in an economy's productive capacity. This growth
can be achieved through several factors:
1. Technological Advancements: Innovations can enhance
efficiency, allowing more output with the same resources.
COzk 2. Increased Resources: A growing labor force or the discovery of
BL- Understand and new natural resources can expand production capabilities. [10]
Apply 3. Investment in Capital: Greater investment in physical capital,

such as machinery and infrastructure, can boost productivity.

4. Improvement in Human Capital: Education and training
enhance worker skills, leading to more efficient production
processes.

As the PPC shifts outward, the economy can produce more of both
goods, leading to improved living standards, reduced unemployment,
and overall economic welfare. However, it's essential for policymakers

to balance resource allocation between different sectors to sustain this
growth effectively.
40 mini

b. | Evaluate the different types of elasticity of demand (price, income, and

cross elasticity) and their practical implications for business decision-
making.

Elasticity of demand measures how responsive the quantity demanded

CO- 1; BL- of a good is to changes in various factors. The main types include:
Understand and 1. Price Elasticity of Demand (PED): This measures the [10]
Evaluate responsiveness of quantity demanded to changes in the price of

the good itself. If demand is elastic (PED > 1), a price decrease

can lead to a significant increase in sales, while an inelastic

demand (PED < 1) indicates that price changes have little effect

on quantity sold. Businesses can use this information to optimize

pricing strategies.
--- page 4 ---
2. Income Elasticity of Demand (YED): This assesses how the
quantity demanded changes in response to changes in consumer
income. Normal goods have positive YED, meaning demand
increases as income rises, while inferior goods have negative
YED. Understanding YED helps businesses target their products
effectively based on income trends and consumer behavior.

3. Cross Elasticity of Demand (XED): This measures the
responsiveness of demand for one good to changes in the price
of another good. A positive XED indicates substitute goods,
while a negative XED indicates complementary goods.
Businesses can leverage this information to set pricing strategies
and marketing efforts to either compete with substitutes or
enhance the sales of complementary products.

Overall, understanding these elasticities allows businesses to make
informed decisions regarding pricing, product development, and market
targeting, ultimately enhancing profitability and market position.

Q3

CO-1&2; BL-
Understand &
Analyze

Analyze the relationship between marginal utility and total utility, and
explain how the law of diminishing marginal utility impacts consumer
behavior.

Marginal utility refers to the additional satisfaction or benefit a
consumer derives from consuming one more unit of a good or service,
while total utility is the overall satisfaction gained from all units
consumed. The relationship between the two is that total utility increases
as more units are consumed, but at a decreasing rate due to the law of
diminishing marginal utility.

According to this law, as a consumer consumes additional units of a
good, the marginal utility gained from each subsequent unit tends to
decrease. For instance, the first slice of pizza may provide significant
satisfaction, but the third or fourth slice may yield much less pleasure.
This decline in marginal utility influences consumer behavior by
affecting their purchasing decisions; consumers are less willing to pay
high prices for additional units once the added satisfaction diminishes.
As a result, consumers allocate their budgets across different goods to
maximize total utility. Understanding this relationship helps businesses
set pricing strategies and marketing approaches, ensuring they align with

[10]
--- page 5 ---
consumer preferences and behavior, ultimately driving sales and
enhancing customer satisfaction.

CO-1&2; b.

Critically assess the impact of the New Economic Policy (Liberalization,
BL- Evaluate

Privatization, and Globalization) on India’s economic growth,
identifying both benefits and limitations.

The New Economic Policy (NEP) introduced in 1991, emphasizing
Liberalization, Privatization, and Globalization (LPG), has had a
significant impact on India's economic growth. One of the primary
benefits of the NEP has been the acceleration of economic growth rates, .
with GDP growth increasing from around 3-4% in the pre-reform era to
approximately’ 6-8% in subsequent years. Liberalization reduced
government control, fostering a competitive environment and
encouraging foreign direct investment (FDI), which led to technology
transfer and job creation.

Privatization of state-owned enterprises improved efficiency and | [10]
productivity by introducing market-driven principles. Globalization
opened Indian markets. to international trade, increasing export
opportunities and enhancing consumer choices.

However, the NEP also has limitations. It contributed to rising income
inequality, with wealth becoming concentrated among certain segments
of society. Additionally, while urban areas saw significant growth, rural
regions and agriculture lagged, leading to regional disparities.
Moreover, the rapid pace of globalization exposed Indian industries to
international competition, which sometimes harmed local businesses.
Thus, while the NEP catalyzed economic growth, it also highlighted the

need for inclusive policies to address its challenges and ensure equitable
development.

Q4 [20]

a. |The Economic Times recently reported that as many as 458
infrastructure projects in India have been hit by a cost overrun of 25.71
é i i tribut
CO- 263: BL- lakh crores. Evaluate how planning failures contribute to such cost

overruns.
Understand &

[10]
Evaluate

Planning failures significantly contribute to cost overruns in
infrastructure projects, as evidenced by the recent report of 458 projects
in India facing a cumulative overrun of %5.71 lakh crores. One primary
--- page 6 ---
factor is inadequate project feasibility studies, which can lead to
unrealistic budgets and timelines. Poorly defined project scopes often
result in scope creep, where additional features and changes are
implemented without adjusting the budget or schedule, escalating costs.
Additionally, insufficient risk assessment and management during the
planning phase can leave projects vulnerable to unforeseen challenges,
such as regulatory hurdles or environmental issues, which can further
inflate expenses. Lack of coordination among stakeholders, including
government agencies, contractors, and local communities, can lead to
delays and miscommunication, complicating project execution.
Moreover, reliance on outdated or overly optimistic cost estimates may
not account for inflation or fluctuations in material prices, leading to
financial shortfalls. Overall, effective planning, including thorough
feasibility studies, clear scope definition, risk management, and
stakeholder engagement, is essential to mitigate these issues and
minimize cost overruns in infrastructure projects.

b. | Analyze the application of any five of Henry Fayol's management
principles in modern organizations, highlighting how they contribute to
effective management practices today.

Henry Fayol's management principles continue to hold relevance in
modern organizations, contributing significantly to effective
management practices. Here are five key principles:
1. Division of Work: This principle promotes specialization,
allowing employees to focus on specific tasks. In modern
organizations, this enhances efficiency and productivity, as

CO-3; BL- workers develop expertise in their roles.
Understand & 2. Authority and Responsibility: Fayol emphasized that authority | [10]
Analyze should come with corresponding responsibility. Today, this

principle fosters accountability within teams, ensuring that
leaders are empowered to make decisions while being
answerable for outcomes.

3. Unity of Command: This principle asserts that employees
should report to one superior to avoid confusion and conflicts. In
contemporary organizational structures, clear reporting lines
enhance communication and streamline decision-making
processes.

4. Centralization: While modern organizations often adopt
decentralized structures, a balanced approach to centralization
--- page 7 ---
ensures that strategic decisions align with organizational goals
while empowering lower levels to make operational choices.
This balance enhances responsiveness and agility.

5. Stability of Tenure: Fayol highlighted the importance of
employee retention for organizational stability. Today,
organizations that focus on employee development and job
security foster loyalty, reducing turnover costs and enhancing
team cohesion.

By applying these principles, modern organizations can cultivate a
structured, accountable, and efficient management framework,

ultimately driving better performance and achieving strategic objectives.

= (20)

a. What is Market Structure? How is it classified based on its distinct
characteristics?

Market structure refers to the organizational characteristics of a market
that influence the behavior of firms and the nature of competition within
that market. It is classified based on several distinct characteristics,
including the number of firms, the type of products offered, barriers to
entry, and the degree of market power.

1. Perfect Competition: Characterized by a large number of small
firms selling identical products, with no single firm able to
influence market prices. Entry and exit barriers are minimal.

CO- 1; BL- Analyze 2. Monopolistic Competition: Involves many firms offering | [10]
differentiated products, allowing for some degree of pricing
power. Firms compete on factors like quality and branding, with
low barriers to entry.

3. Oligopoly: Consists of a few large firms dominating the market,
offering either homogeneous or differentiated products. Firms
are interdependent, meaning the actions of one firm significantly
impact others, and barriers to entry are high.

4. Monopoly: Features a single firm that controls the entire market
for a product or service, leading to significant pricing power.

High barriers to entry prevent other firms from entering the
market.
--- page 8 ---
Understanding these market structures helps businesses formulate
strategies, set prices, and predict competitive behavior, ultimately
guiding their operations and long-term planning.

b. | Explain how Maslow's theory of motivation can be applied within an
organization to achieve workplace success.

Maslow's theory of motivation is like the ultimate roadmap for keeping
employees engaged and productive. It’s based on a hierarchy of needs,
starting from the basics (survival stuff) to more complex needs (like
personal growth). Here’s how it works in the office:
1. Physiological Needs: Give people the essentials—fair pay,
comfortable workspaces, and access to coffee
2. Safety Needs: Job security is key. People need to know they

CO-3: aren’t going to get axed out of nowhere. Clear policies, benefits,

: Pies
BL Remehiber & and a oh environment check this box. [10]
‘Nepl 3. Belongingness: We all need our office squad! Encouraging

PP'Y teamwork, fostering good vibes, and maybe throwing in a team

lunch helps people feel connected.

4. Esteem Needs: Shout out to the hard workers! Recognition,
promotions, and praise make people feel valued and motivated
to keep crushing it.

5. Self-Actualization: This is where employees want to grow and
realize their potential. Offering training, growth opportunities,
and creative freedom helps them hit this level.

So, applying Maslow's theory basically means fulfilling these levels, and
your team will be thriving, happy, and productive!

bas ; [20]

a. | How does a producer achieve equilibrium using the least-cost
combination of inputs?

A producer achieves equilibrium using the least-cost combination of

CO-1; inputs by optimizing resource allocation to minimize production costs
; sake tees : a f [10]

BL- Analyze while maintaining a given level of output. This is done by ensuring that

the marginal product per unit cost of each input is equal across all

inputs. In other words, the producer compares the additional output

generated by each input (labor, capital, etc.) relative to its cost and

adjusts input quantities to equalize their ratios.
--- page 9 ---
This equilibrium condition is mathematically expressed as:

By achieving this balance, the producer ensures that no resources are
wasted, and production is being carried out at the lowest possible cost
for a given level of output.

This approach also aligns with isoquant and isocost curves, where the
producer aims to produce on the lowest isocost line that still touches the
isoquant representing the desired output level. This ensures maximum
efficiency and cost-effectiveness in production.

b. | Analyze the process of controlling in an organization and discuss the
essentials of a good control system.

The process of controlling in an organization involves monitoring
performance, comparing it against established standards, and taking
corrective actions when necessary to ensure that organizational goals are
met. It is a continuous process consisting of four key steps:

1. Setting Performance Standards: Clear, measurable, and
achievable standards are established based on organizational
objectives.

2. Measuring Actual Performance: Regularly tracking and
evaluating actual performance using reports, data, and metrics.

3. Comparing Performance Against Standards: Comparing the
measured performance with the set standards to identify any
deviations.

COS, El Remember 4. Taking Corrective Actions: If deviations occur, corrective | [10]
and Analyze : ; é ‘
actions are implemented to align performance with the standards.
A good control system has several essentials:

e Accuracy: Controls must provide reliable information for
decision-making.

¢ Timeliness: Controls should offer timely feedback, allowing for
quick responses.

¢ Flexibility: The system should adapt to changing conditions and
requirements.

e Cost-effectiveness: The benefits of control should outweigh its
costs.

e Clarity: Clear standards and performance measures ensure
understanding across the organization.

Effective control ensures that resources are used efficiently, goals are

achieved, and deviations are quickly addressed, contributing to
organizational success.

Q7

[20]

CO-2&3; BL-
Understand and
Analyze

Identify and analyze the barriers to effective decision-making within an
organization. How can these barriers be mitigated to improve the
decision-making process?

Barriers to effective decision-making in organizations include several
factors that hinder the process: Ma

1. Information Overload: Excessive data can overwhelm
decision-makers, leading to confusion or delayed decisions.
Prioritizing key information and using data analysis tools can
mitigate this issue.

2. Uncertainty: Lack of clear information or unpredictable
outcomes can lead to indecisiveness. Conducting thorough risk
assessments and scenario planning helps reduce uncertainty.

3. Bias and Subjectivity: Personal biases, such as confirmation
bias or overconfidence, can distort rational decision-making.
Encouraging diverse perspectives and using objective criteria
can counteract these biases.

4. Groupthink: In group settings, the desire for consensus may
suppress dissenting opinions, leading to flawed decisions.
Promoting open dialogue and allowing critical feedback helps
prevent groupthink.

5. Time Constraints: Rushed decisions due to tight deadlines may
result in poor judgment. Proper time management and setting
realistic deadlines can alleviate this pressure.

6. Resistance to Change: Fear of failure or reluctance to change
can impede decision-making. Creating a culture that supports
innovation and rewards risk-taking can reduce this resistance.

To improve decision-making, organizations should foster a transparent
and open communication environment, ensure access to accurate data,
promote collaborative decision-making, and encourage analytical
thinking to navigate these barriers effectively.

[10]

CO- 1; BL-
Understand

Discuss the importance of cost analysis for business with suitable
examples.

Cost analysis is critical for businesses as it helps in understanding,
managing, and reducing costs to maximize profitability and efficiency.

[10]

10
--- page 11 ---
| By evaluating the costs associated with production, operations, and
marketing, businesses can make informed decisions on pricing,
budgeting, and resource allocation.
For example, fixed costs (like rent and salaries) remain constant
regardless of output, while variable costs (like raw materials) fluctuate
with production levels. Understanding these distinctions helps
businesses forecast profits and adjust operations accordingly. In
manufacturing, for instance, cost analysis helps determine the most cost-
effective production methods, whether to automate processes or hire
additional labor.
Break-even analysis is another important aspect, helping businesses
understand how many units they need to sell to cover costs. For example,
a company producing smartphones might calculate that selling 10,000
units is necessary to cover fixed and variable costs, guiding its pricing
strategy and production targets.
By regularly conducting cost. analysis, businesses can identify

inefficiencies, set competitive pricing, and ensure long-term financial
sustainability.
